style="font-size:114%;margin:0 4em"><span style="line-height:1.6em"><a href="/wiki/Ecuadorian%E2%80%93Peruvian_territorial_dispute" title="Ecuadorian–Peruvian territorial dispute"><span class="wrap">Ecuadorian–Peruvian territorial dispute</span></a></span></div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="navbox-list navbox-odd hlist" style="width:100%;padding:0"><div style="padding:0 0.25em">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Gran_Colombia%E2%80%93Peru_War" title="Gran Colombia–Peru War">Gran Colombia–Peru War (1828–1829)</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ecuadorian%E2%80%93Peruvian_War_(1857%E2%80%931860)" title="Ecuadorian–Peruvian War (1857–1860)">Ecuadorian–Peruvian War (1857–1860)</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Ecuadorian%E2%80%93Peruvian_War" title="Ecuadorian–Peruvian War">Ecuadorian–Peruvian War (1941)</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Paquisha_War" title="Paquisha War">Paquisha War (1981)</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Cenepa_War" title="Cenepa War">Cenepa War (1995)</a></li></ul>
<hr />
<p><a href="/wiki/Brasilia_Presidential_Act" title="Brasilia Presidential Act">Brasilia Presidential Act</a>
</p>
</div></td></tr></tbody></table></div>
<p>After Ecuador's separation from Colombia on 13 May 1830, its first President, General <a href="/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_Flores" title="Juan José Flores">Juan José Flores</a>, laid claim to the territory that had belonged to the <a href="/wiki/Real_Audiencia_of_Quito" title="Real Audiencia of Quito">Real Audiencia of Quito</a>, also referred to as the Presidencia of Quito. He supported his claims with Spanish Royal decrees, or <i>real cedulas</i>, that delineated the borders of Spain's former overseas colonies. In the case of Ecuador, Flores based Ecuador's <i><a href="/wiki/De_jure" title="De jure">de jure</a></i> claims on the Real Cedulas of 1563, 1739, and 1740; with modifications in the Amazon Basin and Andes Mountains that were introduced through the <a href="/wiki/Treaty_of_Guayaquil" title="Treaty of Guayaquil">Treaty of Guayaquil</a> (1829) which Peru reluctantly signed, after the overwhelmingly outnumbered Gran Colombian force led by <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Jos%C3%A9_de_Sucre" title="Antonio José de Sucre">Antonio José de Sucre</a> defeated President and General La Mar's Peruvian invasion force in the <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tarqui" title="Battle of Tarqui">Battle of Tarqui</a>. In addition, Ecuador's eastern border with the Portuguese colony of Brazil in the Amazon Basin was modified before the Wars of Independence by the <a href="/wiki/First_Treaty_of_San_Ildefonso" title="First Treaty of San Ildefonso">First Treaty of San Ildefonso</a> (1777) between the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Empire" title="Spanish Empire">Spanish Empire</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_Empire" title="Portuguese Empire">Portuguese Empire</a>. Moreover, to add legitimacy to his claims, on 16 February 1840, Flores signed a treaty with Spain, whereby Flores convinced Spain to officially recognize Ecuadorian independence and its sole rights to colonial titles over Spain's former colonial territory known anciently to Spain as the Kingdom and Presidency of Quito.
</p><p>Ecuador during its long and turbulent history has lost most of its contested territories to each of its more powerful neighbors, such as Colombia in 1832 and 1916, Brazil in 1904 through a series of peaceful treaties, and Peru after a short war in which the Protocol of Rio de Janeiro was signed in 1942.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Struggle_for_independence">Struggle for independence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Struggle for independence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>During the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_American_wars_of_independence" title="Spanish American wars of independence">struggle for independence</a>, before Peru or Ecuador became independent nations, a few areas of the former Vice Royalty of New Granada – Guayaquil, Tumbez, and Jaén – declared themselves independent from Spain. A few months later, a part of the Peruvian liberation army of San Martin decided to occupy the independent cities of Tumbez and Jaén with the intention of using these towns as springboards to occupy the independent city of Guayaquil and then to liberate the rest of the Audiencia de Quito (Ecuador). It was common knowledge among the top officers of the liberation army from the south that their leader <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_San_Mart%C3%ADn" title="José de San Martín">San Martin</a> wished to liberate present-day Ecuador and add it to the future republic of Peru, since it had been part of the Inca Empire before the Spaniards conquered it. However, <a href="/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar" title="Simón Bolívar">Bolívar</a>'s intention was to form a new republic known as the Gran Colombia, out of the liberated Spanish territory of New Granada which consisted of Colombia, Venezuela, and Ecuador. San Martin's plans were thwarted when Bolívar, with the help of Marshal Antonio José de Sucre and the Gran Colombian liberation force, descended from the Andes mountains and occupied Guayaquil; they also annexed the newly liberated Audiencia de Quito to the Republic of <a href="/wiki/Gran_Colombia" title="Gran Colombia">Gran Colombia</a>.
</p><p>In the south, Ecuador had <i>de jure</i> claims to a small piece of land beside the Pacific Ocean known as <a href="/wiki/Tumbes_Region" class="mw-redirect" title="Tumbes Region">Tumbes</a> which lay between the <a href="/wiki/Zarumilla_River" title="Zarumilla River">Zarumilla</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tumbes_River" title="Tumbes River">Tumbes</a> rivers. In Ecuador's southern Andes Mountain region where the Marañon cuts across, Ecuador had <i>de jure</i> claims to an area it called <a href="/wiki/Ja%C3%A9n,_Peru" title="Jaén, Peru">Jaén de Bracamoros</a>. These areas were included as part of the territory of Gran Colombia by Bolivar on 17 December 1819, during the <a href="/wiki/Congress_of_Angostura" title="Congress of Angostura">Congress of Angostura</a> when the Republic of Gran Colombia was created. Tumbes declared itself independent from Spain on 17 January 1821, and Jaen de Bracamoros on 17 June 1821, without any outside help from revolutionary armies. However, that same year, 1821, Peruvian forces participating in the Trujillo revolution occupied both Jaen and Tumbes. Some Peruvian generals, without any legal titles backing them up and with Ecuador still federated with the Gran Colombia, had the desire to annex Ecuador to the Republic of Peru at the expense of the Gran Colombia, feeling that Ecuador was once part of the Inca Empire.
</p><p>On 28 July 1821, Peruvian independence was proclaimed in Lima by San Martin, and Tumbes and Jaen, which were included as part of the revolution of Trujillo by the Peruvian occupying force, had the whole region swear allegiance to the new Peruvian flag and incorporated itself into Peru. Gran Colombia had always protested Peru for the return of Jaen and Tumbes for almost a decade, then finally Bolivar after long and futile discussion over the return of Jaen, Tumbes, and part of Mainas, declared war. President and General <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_de_La_Mar" class="mw-redirect" title="José de La Mar">José de La Mar</a>, who was born in Ecuador, believing his opportunity had come to annex the District of Ecuador to Peru, personally, with a Peruvian force, invaded and occupied Guayaquil and a few cities in the Loja region of southern Ecuador on 28 November 1828.
</p><p>The war ended when a triumphant heavily outnumbered southern Gran Colombian army at <a href="/wiki/Battle_of_Tarqui" title="Battle of Tarqui">Battle of Tarqui</a> dated 27 February 1829, led by <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Jos%C3%A9_de_Sucre" title="Antonio José de Sucre">Antonio José de Sucre</a>, defeated the Peruvian invasion force led by President La Mar. This defeat led to the signing of the Treaty of Guayaquil dated 22 September 1829, whereby Peru and its Congress recognized Gran Colombian rights over Tumbes, Jaen, and Maynas. Through protocolized meetings between representatives of Peru and Gran Colombia, the border was set as Tumbes river in the west and in the east the Maranon and Amazon rivers were to be followed toward Brazil as the most natural borders between them. However, what was pending was whether the new border around the Jaen region should follow the Chinchipe River or the Huancabamba River. According to the peace negotiations Peru agreed to return Guayaquil, Tumbez, and Jaén; despite this, Peru returned Guayaquil, but failed to return Tumbes and Jaén, alleging that it was not obligated to follow the agreements, since the Gran Colombia ceased to exist when it divided itself into three different nations – Ecuador, Colombia, and Venezuela.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:AGHRC_(1890)_-_Carta_XI_-_Divisi%C3%B3n_pol%C3%ADtica_de_Colombia,_1824.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/AGHRC_%281890%29_-_Carta_XI_-_Divisi%C3%B3n_pol%C3%ADtica_de_Colombia%2C_1824.jpg/220px-AGHRC_%281890%29_-_Carta_XI_-_Divisi%C3%B3n_pol%C3%ADtica_de_Colombia%2C_1824.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="177" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/AGHRC_%281890%29_-_Carta_XI_-_Divisi%C3%B3n_pol%C3%ADtica_de_Colombia%2C_1824.jpg/330px-AGHRC_%281890%29_-_Carta_XI_-_Divisi%C3%B3n_pol%C3%ADtica_de_Colombia%2C_1824.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/AGHRC_%281890%29_-_Carta_XI_-_Divisi%C3%B3n_pol%C3%ADtica_de_Colombia%2C_1824.jpg/440px-AGHRC_%281890%29_-_Carta_XI_-_Divisi%C3%B3n_pol%C3%ADtica_de_Colombia%2C_1824.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4293" data-file-height="3452" /></a><figcaption>Map of the former <a href="/wiki/Gran_Colombia" title="Gran Colombia">Gran Colombia</a> in 1824 (named in its time as Colombia), the Gran Colombia covered all the colored region.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The Central District of the Gran Colombia, known as Cundinamarca or New Granada (modern Colombia) with its capital in Bogota, did not recognize the separation of the Southern District of the Gran Colombia, with its capital in Quito, from the Gran Colombian federation on 13 May 1830. After Ecuador's separation, the <a href="/wiki/Department_of_Cauca" class="mw-redirect" title="Department of Cauca">Department of Cauca</a> voluntarily decided to unite itself with Ecuador due to instability in the central government of Bogota. The Venezuelan born President of Ecuador, the general <a href="/wiki/Juan_Jos%C3%A9_Flores" title="Juan José Flores">Juan José Flores</a>, with the approval of the Ecuadorian congress annexed the Department of Cauca on 20 December 1830, since the government of Cauca had called for union with the District of the South as far back as April 1830. Moreover, the Cauca region, throughout its long history, had very strong economic and cultural ties with the people of Ecuador. Also, the Cauca region, which included such cities as <a href="/wiki/Pasto,_Colombia" title="Pasto, Colombia">Pasto</a>, <a href="/wiki/Popay%C3%A1n" title="Popayán">Popayán</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Buenaventura,_Valle_del_Cauca" title="Buenaventura, Valle del Cauca">Buenaventura</a>, had always been dependent on the Presidencia or Audiencia of Quito.
</p><p>Fruitless negotiations continued between the governments of Bogotá and Quito, where the government of Bogotá did not recognize the separation of Ecuador or that of Cauca from the Gran Colombia until war broke out in May 1832. In five months, New Granada defeated Ecuador due to the fact that the majority of the Ecuadorian Armed Forces were composed of rebellious angry unpaid veterans from Venezuela and Colombia that did not want to fight against their fellow countrymen. Seeing that his officers were rebelling, mutinying, and changing sides, President Flores had no option but to reluctantly make peace with New Granada. The Treaty of Pasto of 1832 was signed by which the Department of Cauca was turned over to New Granada (modern Colombia), the government of Bogotá recognized Ecuador as an independent country and the border was to follow the Ley de División Territorial de la República de Colombia (Law of the Division of Territory of the Gran Colombia) passed on 25 June 1824. This law set the border at the river Carchi and the eastern border that stretched to Brazil at the Caquetá river. Later, Ecuador contended that the Republic of Colombia, while reorganizing its government, unlawfully made its eastern border provisional and that Colombia extended its claims south to the Napo River because it said that the Government of Popayán extended its control all the way to the Napo River.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Struggle_for_possession_of_the_Amazon_Basin">Struggle for possession of the Amazon Basin</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Struggle for possession of the Amazon Basin">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:South_America_1879.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/South_America_1879.png/220px-South_America_1879.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="363" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/South_America_1879.png/330px-South_America_1879.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3b/South_America_1879.png/440px-South_America_1879.png 2x" data-file-width="465" data-file-height="768" /></a><figcaption>South America (1879): All land claims by Peru, Ecuador, Colombia, Brazil, Argentina, Chile, and Bolivia in 1879</figcaption></figure>
<p>When Ecuador seceded from the Gran Colombia, Peru contested Ecuador's claims with the newly discovered <i>Real Cedula</i> of 1802, by which Peru claims the King of Spain had transferred these lands from the Viceroyalty of New Granada to the Viceroyalty of Peru. During colonial times this was to halt the ever-expanding Portuguese settlements into Spanish domains, which were left vacant and in disorder after the expulsion of Jesuit missionaries from their bases along the Amazon Basin. Ecuador countered by labeling the Cedula of 1802 an ecclesiastical instrument, which had nothing to do with political borders. Peru began its de facto occupation of disputed Amazonian territories, after it signed a secret 1851 peace treaty in favor of Brazil. This treaty disregarded Spanish rights that were confirmed during colonial times by a Spanish-Portuguese treaty over the Amazon regarding territories held by illegal Portuguese settlers.
</p><p>Peru began occupying the missionary villages in the Mainas or Maynas region, which it began calling Loreto, with its capital in <a href="/wiki/Iquitos" title="Iquitos">Iquitos</a>. During its negotiations with Brazil, Peru claimed Amazonian Basin territories up to Caqueta River in the north and toward the Andes Mountain range. Colombia protested stating that its claims extended south toward the Napo and Amazon Rivers. Ecuador protested that it claimed the Amazon Basin between the Caqueta river and the Marañon-Amazon river. Peru ignored these protests and created the Department of Loreto in 1853 with its capital in Iquitos. Peru briefly occupied Guayaquil again in 1860, since Peru thought that Ecuador was selling some of the disputed land for development to British bond holders, but returned Guayaquil after a few months. The border dispute was then submitted to Spain for arbitration from 1880 to 1910, but to no avail.<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the early part of the 20th century, Ecuador made an effort to peacefully define its eastern Amazonian borders with its neighbours through negotiation. On 6 May 1904, Ecuador signed the Tobar-Rio Branco Treaty recognizing Brazil's claims to the Amazon in recognition of Ecuador's claim to be an Amazonian country to counter Peru's earlier Treaty with Brazil back on 23 October 1851. Then after a few meetings with the Colombian government's representatives an agreement was reached and the Muñoz Vernaza-Suarez Treaty was signed 15 July 1916, in which Colombian rights to the Putumayo river were recognized as well as Ecuador's rights to the Napo river and the new border was a line that ran midpoint between those two rivers. In this way, Ecuador gave up the claims it had to the Amazonian territories between the Caquetá River and Napo River to Colombia, thus cutting itself off from Brazil. Later, a brief war erupted between Colombia and Peru, over Peru's claims to the Caquetá region, which ended with Peru reluctantly signing the Salomon-Lozano Treaty on 24 March 1922. Ecuador protested this secret treaty, since Colombia gave away Ecuadorian claimed land to Peru that Ecuador had given to Colombia in 1916.
</p><p>On 21 July 1924, the Ponce-Castro Oyanguren Protocol was signed between Ecuador and Peru where both agreed to hold direct negotiations and to resolve the dispute in an equitable manner and to submit the differing points of the dispute to the United States for arbitration. Negotiations between the Ecuadorian and Peruvian representatives began in Washington on 30 September 1935. The negotiations turned into intense arguments during the next 7 months and finally on 29 September 1937, the Peruvian representatives decided to break off the negotiations.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Four years later in 1941, amid fast-growing tensions within disputed territories around the Zarumilla River, war broke out with Peru. Peru claimed that Ecuador's military presence in Peruvian-claimed territory was an invasion; Ecuador, for its part, claimed that Peru had recently invaded Ecuador around the Zarumilla River and that Peru since Ecuador's independence from Spain has systematically occupied Tumbez, Jaen, and most of the disputed territories in the Amazonian Basin between the Putomayo and Marañon Rivers. In July 1941, troops were mobilized in both countries. Peru had an army of 11,681 troops who faced a poorly supplied and inadequately armed Ecuadorian force of 2,300, of which only 1,300 were deployed in the southern provinces. Hostilities erupted on 5 July 1941, when Peruvian forces crossed the Zarumilla river at several locations, testing the strength and resolve of the Ecuadorian border troops. Finally, on 23 July 1941, the Peruvians launched a major invasion, crossing the Zarumilla river in force and advancing into the Ecuadorian province of <a href="/wiki/El_Oro_Province" title="El Oro Province">El Oro</a>.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ecuador-peru-land-claims-01.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Ecuador-peru-land-claims-01.png/220px-Ecuador-peru-land-claims-01.png" decoding="async" width="220" height="161" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Ecuador-peru-land-claims-01.png/330px-Ecuador-peru-land-claims-01.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Ecuador-peru-land-claims-01.png/440px-Ecuador-peru-land-claims-01.png 2x" data-file-width="1000" data-file-height="731" /></a><figcaption>Map of Ecuadorian land claims after 1916</figcaption></figure>
<p>During the course of the <a href="/wiki/Ecuadorian%E2%80%93Peruvian_War" title="Ecuadorian–Peruvian War">Ecuadorian–Peruvian War</a>, Peru gained control over part of the disputed territory and some parts of the province of El Oro, and some parts of the <a href="/wiki/Loja_Province" title="Loja Province">province of Loja</a>, demanding that the Ecuadorian government give up its territorial claims. The Peruvian Navy blocked the port of <a href="/wiki/Guayaquil" title="Guayaquil">Guayaquil</a>, almost cutting all supplies to the Ecuadorian troops. After a few weeks of war and under pressure by the United States and several Latin American nations, all fighting came to a stop. Ecuador and Peru came to an accord formalized in the <a href="/wiki/Rio_Protocol" title="Rio Protocol">Rio Protocol</a>, signed on 29 January 1942, in favor of hemispheric unity against the <a href="/wiki/Axis_Powers" class="mw-redirect" title="Axis Powers">Axis Powers</a> in <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a> favoring Peru with the territory they occupied at the time the war came to an end.
</p><p>The 1944 <a href="/w/index.php?title=Glorious_May_Revolution&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Glorious May Revolution (page does not exist)">Glorious May Revolution</a> followed a military-civilian rebellion and a subsequent civic strike which successfully removed Carlos Arroyo del Río as a dictator from Ecuador's government. However, a post-Second World War recession and popular unrest led to a return to populist politics and domestic military interventions in the 1960s, while foreign companies developed oil resources in the Ecuadorian Amazon. In 1972, construction of the Andean pipeline was completed. The pipeline brought oil from the east side of the Andes to the coast, making Ecuador South America's second largest oil exporter.
</p><p>In 1978, the city of <a href="/wiki/Quito" title="Quito">Quito</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Gal%C3%A1pagos_Islands" title="Galápagos Islands">Galápagos Islands</a> were inscribed as <a href="/wiki/UNESCO_World_Heritage_Sites" class="mw-redirect" title="UNESCO World Heritage Sites">UNESCO World Heritage Sites</a>, making the first two properties in the world to become listed sites.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Relevo-ecu.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Relevo-ecu.JPG/220px-Relevo-ecu.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="107" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Relevo-ecu.JPG/330px-Relevo-ecu.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a6/Relevo-ecu.JPG/440px-Relevo-ecu.JPG 2x" data-file-width="557" data-file-height="272" /></a><figcaption>Ecuadorian troops during the <a href="/wiki/Cenepa_War" title="Cenepa War">Cenepa War</a></figcaption></figure>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mirage_F.1JA.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Mirage_F.1JA.JPG/220px-Mirage_F.1JA.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="91" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Mirage_F.1JA.JPG/330px-Mirage_F.1JA.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/22/Mirage_F.1JA.JPG/440px-Mirage_F.1JA.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1700" data-file-height="700" /></a><figcaption>The <a href="/wiki/Dassault_Mirage_F1" title="Dassault Mirage F1">Mirage F.1JA</a> (FAE-806) was one aircraft involved in the claimed shooting down of two Peruvian <a href="/wiki/Sukhoi_Su-22" class="mw-redirect" title="Sukhoi Su-22">Sukhoi Su-22</a> on 10 February 1995.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Rio_Protocol" title="Rio Protocol">Rio Protocol</a> failed to precisely resolve the border along a little river in the remote <i>Cordillera del Cóndor</i> region in southern Ecuador. This caused a long-simmering dispute between Ecuador and Peru, which ultimately led to fighting between the two countries; first a border skirmish in January–February 1981 known as the <a href="/wiki/Paquisha_Incident" class="mw-redirect" title="Paquisha Incident">Paquisha Incident</a>, and ultimately full-scale warfare in January 1995 where the Ecuadorian military shot down Peruvian aircraft and helicopters and Peruvian infantry marched into southern Ecuador. Each country blamed the other for the onset of hostilities, known as the <a href="/wiki/Cenepa_War" title="Cenepa War">Cenepa War</a>. <a href="/wiki/Sixto_Dur%C3%A1n_Ball%C3%A9n" title="Sixto Durán Ballén">Sixto Durán Ballén</a>, the Ecuadorian president, famously declared that he would not give up a single centimeter of Ecuador. Popular sentiment in Ecuador became strongly <a href="/wiki/Nationalism" title="Nationalism">nationalistic</a> against Peru: graffiti could be seen on the walls of Quito referring to Peru as the "<i>Cain de Latinoamérica</i>", a reference to the murder of <a href="/wiki/Abel" title="Abel">Abel</a> by his brother <a href="/wiki/Cain" title="Cain">Cain</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Genesis" title="Book of Genesis">Book of Genesis</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ecuador and Peru signed the <a href="/wiki/Brasilia_Presidential_Act" title="Brasilia Presidential Act">Brasilia Presidential Act</a> peace agreement on 26 October 1998, which ended hostilities, and effectively put an end to the Western Hemisphere's longest running territorial dispute.<sup id="cite_ref-ucdp.uu.se_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ucdp.uu.se-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> The Guarantors of the <a href="/wiki/Rio_Protocol" title="Rio Protocol">Rio Protocol</a> (Argentina, Brazil, Chile, and the United States of America) ruled that the border of the undelineated zone was to be set at the line of the <i>Cordillera del Cóndor</i>. While Ecuador had to give up its decades-old territorial claims to the eastern slopes of the Cordillera, as well as to the entire western area of Cenepa headwaters, Peru was compelled to give to Ecuador, in perpetual lease but without sovereignty, 1&#160;km<sup>2</sup> (0.39&#160;sq&#160;mi) of its territory, in the area where the Ecuadorian base of Tiwinza – focal point of the war – had been located within Peruvian soil and which the Ecuadorian Army held during the conflict. The final border demarcation came into effect on 13 May 1999, and the multi-national MOMEP (Military Observer Mission for Ecuador and Peru) troop deployment withdrew on 17 June 1999.<sup id="cite_ref-ucdp.uu.se_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ucdp.uu.se-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Military_governments_.281972.E2.80.9379.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Military_governments_(1972–79)">Military governments (1972–79)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Military governments (1972–79)">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1972, a "revolutionary and nationalist" military <a href="/wiki/Military_dictatorship" title="Military dictatorship">junta</a> overthrew the government of Velasco Ibarra. The <a href="/wiki/Coup_d%27%C3%A9tat" title="Coup d&#39;état">coup d'état</a> was led by General <a href="/wiki/Guillermo_Rodr%C3%ADguez_(politician)" title="Guillermo Rodríguez (politician)">Guillermo Rodríguez</a> and executed by navy commander Jorge Queirolo G. The new president exiled José María Velasco to Argentina. He remained in power until 1976, when he was removed by another military government. That military junta was led by Admiral <a href="/wiki/Alfredo_Poveda" title="Alfredo Poveda">Alfredo Poveda</a>, who was declared chairman of the Supreme Council. The Supreme Council included two other members: General Guillermo Durán Arcentales and General Luis Pintado. The civil society more and more insistently called for democratic elections. Colonel <a href="/wiki/Richelieu_Levoyer" title="Richelieu Levoyer">Richelieu Levoyer</a>, Government Minister, proposed and implemented a Plan to return to the constitutional system through universal elections. This plan enabled the new democratically elected president to assume the duties of the executive office.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Return_to_democracy">Return to democracy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Return to democracy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/History_of_Ecuador_(1990%E2%80%93present)" title="History of Ecuador (1990–present)">History of Ecuador (1990–present)</a></div>
<p>Elections were held on 29 April 1979, under a new constitution. <a href="/wiki/Jaime_Rold%C3%B3s_Aguilera" title="Jaime Roldós Aguilera">Jaime Roldós Aguilera</a> was elected president, garnering over one million votes, the most in Ecuadorian history. He took office on 10 August, as the first constitutionally elected president after nearly a decade of civilian and military dictatorships. In 1980, he founded the <i>Partido Pueblo, Cambio y Democracia</i> (People, Change, and Democracy Party) after withdrawing from the <i>Concentración de Fuerzas Populares</i> (Popular Forces Concentration) and governed until 24 May 1981, when he died along with his wife and the minister of defense, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Marco_Subia_Martinez&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Marco Subia Martinez (page does not exist)">Marco Subia Martinez</a>, when his Air Force plane crashed in heavy rain near the Peruvian border. Many people believe that he was assassinated by the CIA,<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> given the multiple death threats leveled against him because of his reformist agenda, deaths in automobile crashes of two key witnesses before they could testify during the investigation, and the sometimes contradictory accounts of the incident.
</p><p>Roldos was immediately succeeded by Vice President Osvaldo Hurtado, who was followed in 1984 by <a href="/wiki/Le%C3%B3n_Febres_Cordero" title="León Febres Cordero">León Febres Cordero</a> from the Social Christian Party. <a href="/wiki/Rodrigo_Borja_Cevallos" title="Rodrigo Borja Cevallos">Rodrigo Borja Cevallos</a> of the Democratic Left (Izquierda Democrática, or ID) party won the presidency in 1988, running in the runoff election against <a href="/wiki/Abdal%C3%A1_Bucaram" title="Abdalá Bucaram">Abdalá Bucaram</a> (brother in law of <a href="/wiki/Jaime_Roldos" class="mw-redirect" title="Jaime Roldos">Jaime Roldos</a> and founder of the Ecuadorian Roldosist Party). His government was committed to improving human rights protection and carried out some reforms, notably an opening of Ecuador to foreign trade. The Borja government concluded an accord leading to the disbanding of the small terrorist group, "<a href="/wiki/%C2%A1Alfaro_Vive,_Carajo!" title="¡Alfaro Vive, Carajo!">¡Alfaro Vive, Carajo!</a>" ("Alfaro Lives, Dammit!"), named after <a href="/wiki/Eloy_Alfaro" title="Eloy Alfaro">Eloy Alfaro</a>. However, continuing economic problems undermined the popularity of the ID, and opposition parties gained control of Congress in 1999.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA.jpg/220px-CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA.jpg/330px-CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fe/CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA.jpg/440px-CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5472" data-file-height="3648" /></a><figcaption>President <a href="/wiki/Len%C3%ADn_Moreno" title="Lenín Moreno">Lenín Moreno</a>, first lady <a href="/wiki/Roc%C3%ADo_Gonz%C3%A1lez_Navas" title="Rocío González Navas">Rocío González Navas</a> and his predecessor <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Correa" title="Rafael Correa">Rafael Correa</a>, 3 April 2017</figcaption></figure>
<p>A notable event was the <a href="/wiki/Cenepa_War" title="Cenepa War">Cenepa War</a> fought between Ecuador and Peru in 1995.
</p><p>Ecuador adopted the <a href="/wiki/United_States_dollar" title="United States dollar">United States dollar</a> on 13 April 2000 as its national currency and on 11 September, the country eliminated the <a href="/wiki/Ecuadorian_sucre" title="Ecuadorian sucre">Ecuadorian sucre</a>, in order to stabilize the <a href="/wiki/Economy_of_Ecuador" title="Economy of Ecuador">country's economy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> The US Dollar has been the only official currency of Ecuador since the year 2000.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The emergence of the Amerindian population as an active constituency has added to the democratic volatility of the country in recent years. The population has been motivated by government failures to deliver on promises of land reform, lower unemployment and provision of social services, and historical exploitation by the land-holding elite. Their movement, along with the continuing destabilizing efforts by both the elite and leftist movements, has led to a deterioration of the executive office. The populace and the other branches of government give the president very little political capital, as illustrated by the most recent removal of President <a href="/wiki/Lucio_Guti%C3%A9rrez" title="Lucio Gutiérrez">Lucio Gutiérrez</a> from office by Congress in April 2005.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Vice President <a href="/wiki/Alfredo_Palacio" title="Alfredo Palacio">Alfredo Palacio</a> took his place<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> and remained in office until the presidential <a href="/wiki/Ecuadorian_general_election,_2006" class="mw-redirect" title="Ecuadorian general election, 2006">election of 2006</a>, in which <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Correa" title="Rafael Correa">Rafael Correa</a> gained the presidency.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup> On 15 January 2007, Rafael Correa was sworn in as the <a href="/wiki/President_of_Ecuador" title="President of Ecuador">President of Ecuador</a>. Several left-wing political leaders of Latin America, his future allies, attended the ceremony.<sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The new socialist constitution, endorsed in <a href="/wiki/2008_Ecuadorian_constitutional_referendum" title="2008 Ecuadorian constitutional referendum">2008 referendum</a> (<a href="/wiki/2008_Constitution_of_Ecuador" title="2008 Constitution of Ecuador">2008 Constitution of Ecuador</a>), implemented leftist reforms.<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> In December 2008, president Correa declared Ecuador's <a href="/wiki/National_debt" class="mw-redirect" title="National debt">national debt</a> illegitimate, based on the argument that it was <a href="/wiki/Odious_debt" title="Odious debt">odious debt</a> contracted by corrupt and despotic prior regimes. He announced that the country would default on over $3 billion worth of bonds; he then pledged to fight creditors in <a href="/wiki/International_court" title="International court">international courts</a> and succeeded in reducing the price of outstanding bonds by more than 60%.<sup id="cite_ref-TRN2009Win_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-TRN2009Win-48">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> He brought Ecuador into the <a href="/wiki/Bolivarian_Alliance_for_the_Americas" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolivarian Alliance for the Americas">Bolivarian Alliance for the Americas</a> in June 2009. Correa's administration  succeeded in reducing the high levels of poverty and unemployment in Ecuador.<sup id="cite_ref-Romero_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Romero-49">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Most_Popular_E-mail_Newsletter_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Most_Popular_E-mail_Newsletter-50">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Rafael Correa's three consecutive terms (from 2007 to 2017) were followed by his former Vice President <a href="/wiki/Len%C3%ADn_Moreno" title="Lenín Moreno">Lenín Moreno</a>'s four years as president (2017–21). After being elected in 2017, President <a href="/wiki/Lenin_Moreno" class="mw-redirect" title="Lenin Moreno">Lenin Moreno</a>'s government adopted <a href="/wiki/Economic_liberalism" title="Economic liberalism">economically liberal</a> policies: reduction of <a href="/wiki/Public_spending" class="mw-redirect" title="Public spending">public spending</a>, <a href="/wiki/Trade_liberalization" class="mw-redirect" title="Trade liberalization">trade liberalization</a>, flexibility of the labour code, etc. Ecuador also left the left-wing <a href="/wiki/Bolivarian_Alliance_for_the_Americas" class="mw-redirect" title="Bolivarian Alliance for the Americas">Bolivarian Alliance for the Americas</a> (Alba) in August 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup> The Productive Development Act enshrines an austerity policy, and reduces the development and redistribution policies of the previous mandate. In the area of taxes, the authorities aim to "encourage the return of investors" by granting amnesty to fraudsters and proposing measures to reduce <a href="/wiki/Corporate_tax" title="Corporate tax">tax rates for large companies</a>. In addition, the government waives the right to tax increases in raw material prices and foreign exchange repatriations.<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup> In October 2018, the government of President Lenin Moreno cut diplomatic relations with the <a href="/wiki/Nicol%C3%A1s_Maduro" title="Nicolás Maduro">Maduro</a> administration of Venezuela, a close ally of Rafael Correa.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The relations with the United States improved significantly during the presidency of Lenin Moreno. In February 2020, his visit to Washington was the first meeting between an Ecuadorian and U.S. president in 17 years.<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> In June 2019, Ecuador had agreed to allow US military planes to operate from an airport on the Galapagos Islands.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A <a href="/wiki/2019_Ecuadorian_protests" title="2019 Ecuadorian protests">series of protests</a> began on 3 October 2019 against the end of fuel <a href="/wiki/Subsidy" title="Subsidy">subsidies</a> and <a href="/wiki/Austerity" title="Austerity">austerity measures</a> adopted by <a href="/wiki/President_of_Ecuador" title="President of Ecuador">President of Ecuador</a> <a href="/wiki/Len%C3%ADn_Moreno" title="Lenín Moreno">Lenín Moreno</a> and his administration. On 10 October, protesters overran the capital Quito causing the Government of Ecuador to relocate to <a href="/wiki/Guayaquil" title="Guayaquil">Guayaquil</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> but the government had plans to return to Quito.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> On 14 October 2019, the government restored fuel subsidies and withdrew an austerity package, meaning the end of nearly two weeks of protests.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The 11 April 2021 <a href="/wiki/2021_Ecuadorian_general_election" title="2021 Ecuadorian general election">election</a> run-off vote ended in a win for conservative former banker, <a href="/wiki/Guillermo_Lasso" title="Guillermo Lasso">Guillermo Lasso</a>, taking 52.4% of the vote compared to 47.6% of left-wing economist <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Arauz" title="Andrés Arauz">Andrés Arauz</a>, supported by exiled former president, Rafael Correa. Previously, President-elect Lasso finished second in the 2013 and 2017 presidential elections.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup> On 24 May 2021, Guillermo Lasso was sworn in as the new <a href="/wiki/President_of_Ecuador" title="President of Ecuador">President of Ecuador</a>, becoming the country's first right-wing leader in 14 years.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> However, President Lasso's party <a href="/wiki/CREO_(Ecuadorian_Movement)" class="mw-redirect" title="CREO (Ecuadorian Movement)">CREO</a> Movement, and its ally the Social Christian Party (PSC) secured only 31 parliamentary seats out of 137, while the Union for Hope (UNES) of Andrés Arauz was the strongest parliamentary group with 49 seats, meaning the new president needs support from Izquierda Democrática and the indigenist <a href="/wiki/Pachakutik_Plurinational_Unity_Movement_%E2%80%93_New_Country" title="Pachakutik Plurinational Unity Movement – New Country">Pachakutik</a> to push through his legislative agenda.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In October 2021, President Lasso declared a 60-day state of emergency with the intention to combat crime and drug-related violence.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> In Ecuador's state prisons there have been numerous bloody clashes between rival groups of prisoners.<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Lasso proposed a series of constitutional changes to enhance his government's ability to respond to rising, largely drug-related crime. In a <a href="/wiki/2023_Ecuadorian_constitutional_referendum" title="2023 Ecuadorian constitutional referendum">referendum</a> in February 2023, voters overwhelmingly rejected his proposed changes. This result weakened Lasso's political standing.<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Government_and_politics">Government and politics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Government and politics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Politics_of_Ecuador" title="Politics of Ecuador">Politics of Ecuador</a></div>
<p>The Ecuadorian State consists of five branches of government: the <i>Executive Branch</i>, the <i>Legislative Branch</i>, the <i>Judicial Branch</i>, the <i>Electoral Branch</i>, and <i>Transparency and Social Control</i>.
</p><p>Ecuador is governed by a democratically elected president, for a four-year term. The president of Ecuador, exercises his power from the presidential <a href="/wiki/Palacio_de_Carondelet" title="Palacio de Carondelet">Palacio de Carondelet</a> in <a href="/wiki/Quito" title="Quito">Quito</a>. The <a href="/wiki/2008_Constitution_of_Ecuador" title="2008 Constitution of Ecuador">current constitution</a> was written by the <a href="/wiki/Ecuadorian_Constituent_Assembly" title="Ecuadorian Constituent Assembly">Ecuadorian Constituent Assembly</a> elected in 2007, and was approved by referendum in 2008. Since 1936, voting is compulsory for all literate persons aged 18–65, optional for all other citizens.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The executive branch includes 23 ministries. Provincial governors and councilors (mayors, aldermen, and parish boards) are directly elected. The <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Ecuador" class="mw-redirect" title="National Assembly of Ecuador">National Assembly of Ecuador</a> meets throughout the year except for recesses in July and December. There are thirteen permanent committees. Members of the National Court of Justice are appointed by the National Judicial Council for nine-year terms.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Executive_branch">Executive branch</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Executive branch">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/List_of_heads_of_state_of_Ecuador" class="mw-redirect" title="List of heads of state of Ecuador">List of heads of state of Ecuador</a></div>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA_(20469640211).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA_%2820469640211%29.jpg/250px-CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA_%2820469640211%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="160" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA_%2820469640211%29.jpg/375px-CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA_%2820469640211%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ef/CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA_%2820469640211%29.jpg/500px-CAMBIO_DE_GUARDIA_%2820469640211%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1963" data-file-height="1260" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Palacio_de_Carondelet" title="Palacio de Carondelet">Palacio de Carondelet</a>, the executive branch of the Ecuadorian Government</figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Executive_(government)" title="Executive (government)">executive branch</a> is led by the president. The president is accompanied by the vice-president, elected for four years (with the ability to be re-elected only once). As head of state and chief government official, he is responsible for public administration including the appointing of national coordinators, ministers, ministers of State and public servants. The executive branch defines foreign policy, appoints the Chancellor of the Republic, as well as ambassadors and consuls, being the ultimate authority over the <a href="/wiki/Military_of_Ecuador" class="mw-redirect" title="Military of Ecuador">Armed Forces of Ecuador</a>, <a href="/wiki/National_Police_of_Ecuador" title="National Police of Ecuador">National Police of Ecuador</a>, and appointing authorities. The acting president's wife receives the title of <a href="/wiki/First_Lady_of_Ecuador" class="mw-redirect" title="First Lady of Ecuador">First Lady of Ecuador</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Legislative_branch">Legislative branch</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Legislative branch">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_(Ecuador)" title="National Assembly (Ecuador)">National Assembly (Ecuador)</a></div>
<p>The legislative branch is embodied by the <a href="/wiki/National_Assembly_of_Ecuador" class="mw-redirect" title="National Assembly of Ecuador">National Assembly</a>, which is headquartered in the city of <a href="/wiki/Quito" title="Quito">Quito</a> in the Legislative Palace, and consists of 137 assemblymen, divided into ten committees and elected for a four-year term. Fifteen national constituency elected assembly, two Assembly members elected from each province and one for every 100,000 inhabitants or fraction exceeding 150,000, according to the latest national population census. In addition, statute determines the election of assembly of regions and metropolitan districts.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Judicial_branch">Judicial branch</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Judicial branch">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Ecuador's judiciary has as its main body the Judicial Council, and also includes the National Court of Justice, provincial courts, and lower courts. Legal representation is made by the Judicial Council.
The National Court of Justice is composed of 21 judges elected for a term of nine years. Judges are renewed by thirds every three years pursuant to the Judicial Code. These are elected by the Judicial Council on the basis of opposition proceedings and merits.
The justice system is buttressed by the independent offices of public prosecutor and the public defender. Auxiliary organs are as follows: <a href="/wiki/Civil_law_notary" title="Civil law notary">notaries</a>, court auctioneers, and court receivers. Also there is a special legal regime for Amerindians.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Electoral_branch">Electoral branch</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Electoral branch">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The electoral system functions by authorities which enter only every four years or when <a href="/wiki/Elections_in_Ecuador" title="Elections in Ecuador">elections or referendums</a> occur. Its main functions are to organize, control elections, and punish the infringement of electoral rules. Its main body is the <a href="/wiki/National_Electoral_Council_(Ecuador)" title="National Electoral Council (Ecuador)">National Electoral Council</a>, which is based in the city of Quito, and consists of seven members of the <a href="/wiki/Political_parties_in_Ecuador" class="mw-redirect" title="Political parties in Ecuador">political parties</a> most voted, enjoying complete financial and administrative autonomy. This body, along with the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Electoral_Tribunal&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Electoral Tribunal (page does not exist)">electoral court</a>, forms the <i>Electoral Branch</i> which is one of Ecuador's five <a href="/wiki/Separation_of_powers" title="Separation of powers">branches of government</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Transparency_and_social_control_branch">Transparency and social control branch</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Transparency and social control branch">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Transparency and Social Control consists of the Council of Citizen Participation and Social Control, an <a href="/wiki/Ombudsman" title="Ombudsman">ombudsman</a>, the Comptroller General of the State, and the superintendents. Branch members hold office for five years. This branch is responsible for promoting transparency and control plans publicly, as well as plans to design mechanisms to combat corruption, as also designate certain authorities, and be the regulatory mechanism of accountability in the country.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_affairs">Foreign affairs</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Foreign affairs">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Ecuador" title="Foreign relations of Ecuador">Foreign relations of Ecuador</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Secretary_Pompeo_Holds_Joint_Press_Conference_with_President_Moreno_(48336351591).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Secretary_Pompeo_Holds_Joint_Press_Conference_with_President_Moreno_%2848336351591%29.jpg/220px-Secretary_Pompeo_Holds_Joint_Press_Conference_with_President_Moreno_%2848336351591%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Secretary_Pompeo_Holds_Joint_Press_Conference_with_President_Moreno_%2848336351591%29.jpg/330px-Secretary_Pompeo_Holds_Joint_Press_Conference_with_President_Moreno_%2848336351591%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/38/Secretary_Pompeo_Holds_Joint_Press_Conference_with_President_Moreno_%2848336351591%29.jpg/440px-Secretary_Pompeo_Holds_Joint_Press_Conference_with_President_Moreno_%2848336351591%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5075" data-file-height="3383" /></a><figcaption>Ecuadorian President <a href="/wiki/Len%C3%ADn_Moreno" title="Lenín Moreno">Lenín Moreno</a> with U.S. Secretary of State <a href="/wiki/Mike_Pompeo" title="Mike Pompeo">Mike Pompeo</a>, 20 July 2019</figcaption></figure>
<p>Ecuador joined the <a href="/wiki/Organization_of_Petroleum_Exporting_Countries" class="mw-redirect" title="Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries">Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries</a> (OPEC) in 1973 and suspended its membership in 1992. Under President <a href="/wiki/Rafael_Correa" title="Rafael Correa">Rafael Correa</a>, the country returned to OPEC before leaving again in 2020 under the instruction of President Moreno, citing its desire to increase crude oil importation to gain more revenue.<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Antarctica, Ecuador has maintained a research station for peaceful scientific study as a member nation of the <a href="/wiki/Antarctica_Treaty" class="mw-redirect" title="Antarctica Treaty">Antarctica Treaty</a>. Ecuador has often placed great emphasis on multilateral approaches to international issues. Ecuador is a member of the United Nations (and most of its specialized agencies) and a member of many regional groups, including the <a href="/wiki/Rio_Group" title="Rio Group">Rio Group</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Latin_American_Economic_System" title="Latin American Economic System">Latin American Economic System</a>, the <a href="/w/index.php?title=Latin_American_Energy_Organization&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Latin American Energy Organization (page does not exist)">Latin American Energy Organization</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Latin_American_Integration_Association" title="Latin American Integration Association">Latin American Integration Association</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Andean_Community_of_Nations" class="mw-redirect" title="Andean Community of Nations">Andean Community of Nations</a>, and the Bank of the South (Spanish: <i>Banco del Sur</i> or <i>BancoSur</i>).
</p><p>In 2017, the Ecuadorian parliament adopted a <i>law on human mobility</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/International_Organization_for_Migration" title="International Organization for Migration">International Organization for Migration</a> lauded Ecuador as the first state to have established the promotion of the concept of universal citizenship in its <a href="/wiki/2008_Constitution_of_Ecuador" title="2008 Constitution of Ecuador">constitution</a>, aiming to promote the universal recognition and protection of the human rights of migrants.<sup id="cite_ref-72" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-72">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup> In March 2019, Ecuador withdrew from the <a href="/wiki/Union_of_South_American_Nations" title="Union of South American Nations">Union of South American Nations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-73" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-73">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Human_rights">Human rights</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Human rights">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>A 2003 <a href="/wiki/Amnesty_International" title="Amnesty International">Amnesty International</a> report was critical that there were scarce few prosecutions for human rights violations committed by security forces, and those only in police courts, which are not considered impartial or independent. There are allegations that the security forces routinely torture prisoners. There are reports of prisoners having died while in police custody. Sometimes the legal process can be delayed until the suspect can be released after the time limit for detention without trial is exceeded. Prisons are overcrowded and conditions in detention centers are "abominable".<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Human_Rights_Council" title="United Nations Human Rights Council">UN's Human Rights Council</a>'s (HRC) Universal Periodic Review (UPR) has treated the restrictions on <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_expression" class="mw-redirect" title="Freedom of expression">freedom of expression</a> and efforts to control <a href="/wiki/NGO" class="mw-redirect" title="NGO">NGOs</a> and recommended that Ecuador should stop the criminal sanctions for the expression of opinions, and delay in implementing judicial reforms. Ecuador rejected the recommendation on decriminalization of libel.<sup id="cite_ref-hrw_75-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hrw-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Human_Rights_Watch" title="Human Rights Watch">Human Rights Watch</a> (HRW) former president Correa intimidated journalists and subjected them to "public denunciation and retaliatory litigation". The sentences to journalists  were years of imprisonment and millions of dollars of compensation, even though defendants had been pardoned.<sup id="cite_ref-hrw_75-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hrw-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Correa stated he was only seeking a retraction for slanderous statements.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to HRW, Correa's government weakened the <a href="/wiki/Freedom_of_press" class="mw-redirect" title="Freedom of press">freedom of press</a> and <a href="/wiki/Judicial_independence" title="Judicial independence">independence of the judicial system</a>. In Ecuador's current judicial system, judges are selected in a contest of merits, rather than government appointments. However, the process of selection has been criticized as biased and subjective. In particular, the final interview is said to be given "excessive weighing". Judges and prosecutors that  made decisions in favor of Correa in his lawsuits had received permanent posts, while others with better assessment grades had been rejected.<sup id="cite_ref-hrw_75-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hrw-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Reformahasta2015_77-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Reformahasta2015-77">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Sea_lion_sleeping_on_bench_in_Puerto_Baquerizo_Moreno_2013.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Sea_lion_sleeping_on_bench_in_Puerto_Baquerizo_Moreno_2013.JPG/220px-Sea_lion_sleeping_on_bench_in_Puerto_Baquerizo_Moreno_2013.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="164" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Sea_lion_sleeping_on_bench_in_Puerto_Baquerizo_Moreno_2013.JPG/330px-Sea_lion_sleeping_on_bench_in_Puerto_Baquerizo_Moreno_2013.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/Sea_lion_sleeping_on_bench_in_Puerto_Baquerizo_Moreno_2013.JPG/440px-Sea_lion_sleeping_on_bench_in_Puerto_Baquerizo_Moreno_2013.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2592" data-file-height="1936" /></a><figcaption>Adult <a href="/wiki/Gal%C3%A1pagos_sea_lion" title="Galápagos sea lion">Galápagos sea lion</a> resting on a park bench in <a href="/wiki/Puerto_Baquerizo_Moreno" title="Puerto Baquerizo Moreno">Puerto Baquerizo Moreno</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The laws also forbid articles and media messages that could favor or disfavor some political message or candidate. In the first half of 2012, twenty private TV or radio stations were closed down.<sup id="cite_ref-hrw_75-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hrw-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> People engaging in public protests against environmental and other issues are prosecuted for "terrorism and sabotage", which may lead to an eight-year prison sentence.<sup id="cite_ref-hrw_75-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hrw-75">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Freedom_House" title="Freedom House">Freedom House</a>, restrictions on the media and civil society have decreased since 2017.<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup> In October 2022, the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations" title="United Nations">United Nations</a> expressed concerns about the dire situation in various detention centers and prisons, and the human rights of those deprived of liberty in Ecuador.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Administrative_divisions">Administrative divisions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Administrative divisions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main articles: <a href="/wiki/Provinces_of_Ecuador" title="Provinces of Ecuador">Provinces of Ecuador</a> and <a href="/wiki/Cantons_of_Ecuador" title="Cantons of Ecuador">Cantons of Ecuador</a></div>
<p>Ecuador is divided into 24 <a href="/wiki/Provinces_of_Ecuador" title="Provinces of Ecuador">provinces</a> (Spanish: <i lang="es">provincias</i>), each with its own administrative capital:
</p>
