</tr>

</tbody></table>
</div>
</div>
<p>According to the Ecuadorian National Institute of Statistics and Census, 91.95% of the country's population have a religion, 7.94% are <a href="/wiki/Atheism" title="Atheism">atheists</a> and 0.11% are <a href="/wiki/Agnosticism" title="Agnosticism">agnostics</a>. Among the people who have a religion, 80.44% are <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Ecuador" title="Catholic Church in Ecuador">Catholic</a>, 11.30% are <a href="/wiki/Evangelical" class="mw-redirect" title="Evangelical">Evangelical</a> <a href="/wiki/Protestantism" title="Protestantism">Protestants</a>, 1.29% are <a href="/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses" title="Jehovah&#39;s Witnesses">Jehovah's Witnesses</a> and 6.97% other (mainly Jewish, Buddhists and Latter-day Saints).<sup id="cite_ref-160" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-160">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the rural parts of Ecuador, Amerindian beliefs and Catholicism are sometimes <a href="/wiki/Syncretism" title="Syncretism">syncretized</a> into a local form of <a href="/wiki/Folk_Catholicism" title="Folk Catholicism">folk Catholicism</a>. Most festivals and annual parades are based on religious celebrations, many incorporating a mixture of rites and icons.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2012)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>There is a small number of <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Christians</a>, Amerindian religions, Muslims (see <a href="/wiki/Islam_in_Ecuador" title="Islam in Ecuador">Islam in Ecuador</a>), <a href="/wiki/Buddhist" class="mw-redirect" title="Buddhist">Buddhists</a> and <a href="/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith" title="Baháʼí Faith">Baháʼí</a>. According to their own estimates, <a href="/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints" title="The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints">The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints</a> accounts for about 1.4% of the population, or 211,165 members at the end of 2012.<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> According to their own sources, in 2017 there were 92,752 <a href="/wiki/Jehovah%27s_Witnesses" title="Jehovah&#39;s Witnesses">Jehovah's Witnesses</a> in the country.<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Ecuador" title="History of the Jews in Ecuador">History of the Jews in Ecuador</a> goes back to the 16th and 17th centuries. Until the 20th century the mayority were <a href="/wiki/Sephardic" class="mw-redirect" title="Sephardic">Sephardic</a> with many <a href="/wiki/Anusim" title="Anusim">Anusim</a> (<a href="/wiki/Crypto-Jews" class="mw-redirect" title="Crypto-Jews">Crypto-Jews</a>) among them. Ashkenazi Jews arrived mostly as refugees after the ascendance of <a href="/wiki/Nazism" title="Nazism">National Socialism </a> in Germany in 1933, with 3000 Jews in Ecuador in 1940. At its peak, in 1950, the Jewish population of Ecuador was estimated at 4,000, but then diminished to some 290 around 2020,<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-165" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-165">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup> forming one of the smallest Jewish communities in <a href="/wiki/South_America" title="South America">South America</a>. Nevertheless, this number is declining because young people leave the country for the United States or Israel. Today the Jewish Community of Ecuador (Comunidad Judía del Ecuador) has its seat in <a href="/wiki/Quito" title="Quito">Quito</a>. There are very small communities in <a href="/wiki/Cuenca_(Ecuador)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cuenca (Ecuador)">Cuenca</a>. The "Comunidad de Culto Israelita" reunites the Jews of <a href="/wiki/Guayaquil" title="Guayaquil">Guayaquil</a>. This community works independently from the "Jewish Community of Ecuador" and is composed of only 30 people.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Health">Health</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Health">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Health_in_Ecuador" title="Health in Ecuador">Health in Ecuador</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:EC_Latacunga_Hospital_2012.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/EC_Latacunga_Hospital_2012.jpg/220px-EC_Latacunga_Hospital_2012.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/EC_Latacunga_Hospital_2012.jpg/330px-EC_Latacunga_Hospital_2012.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/EC_Latacunga_Hospital_2012.jpg/440px-EC_Latacunga_Hospital_2012.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1678" data-file-height="1258" /></a><figcaption>IESS Hospital in <a href="/wiki/Latacunga" title="Latacunga">Latacunga</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The current structure of the Ecuadorian public health care system dates back to 1967.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup> The Ministry of the Public Health (Ministerio de Salud Pública del Ecuador) is the responsible entity of the regulation and creation of the public health policies and health care plans. The Minister of Public Health is appointed directly by the President of the Republic.
</p><p>The philosophy of the Ministry of Public Health is the social support and service to the most vulnerable population,<sup id="cite_ref-health_169-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-health-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> and its main plan of action lies around communitarian health and preventive medicine.<sup id="cite_ref-health_169-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-health-169">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup> Many American medical groups often conduct <a href="/wiki/Medical_mission" class="mw-redirect" title="Medical mission">medical missions</a> away from the big cities to provide medical health to poor communities.
</p><p>The public healthcare system allows patients to be treated without an appointment in public general hospitals by general practitioners and specialists in the outpatient clinic (<i>Consulta Externa</i>) at no cost. This is done in the four basic specialties of pediatric, gynecology, clinic medicine, and surgery.<sup id="cite_ref-healths_170-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-healths-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> There are also public hospitals specialized to treat chronic diseases, target a particular group of the population, or provide better treatment in some medical specialties.
</p><p>Although well-equipped general hospitals are found in the major cities or capitals of provinces, there are basic hospitals in the smaller towns and <a href="/wiki/Canton_(country_subdivision)" class="mw-redirect" title="Canton (country subdivision)">canton</a> cities for family care consultation and treatments in pediatrics, gynecology, clinical medicine, and surgery.<sup id="cite_ref-healths_170-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-healths-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Community health care centers (Centros de Salud) are found inside metropolitan areas of cities and in rural areas. These are day hospitals that provide treatment to patients whose hospitalization is under 24 hours.<sup id="cite_ref-healths_170-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-healths-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
The doctors assigned to rural communities, where the Amerindian population can be substantial, have small clinics under their responsibility for the treatment of patients in the same fashion as the day hospitals in the major cities. The treatment in this case respects the culture of the community.<sup id="cite_ref-healths_170-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-healths-170">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The public healthcare system should not be confused with the Ecuadorian Social Security healthcare service, which is dedicated to individuals with formal employment and who are affiliated obligatorily through their employers. Citizens with no formal employment may still contribute to the social security system voluntarily and have access to the medical services rendered by the social security system. The Ecuadorian Institute of Social Security (IESS) has several major hospitals and medical sub-centers under its administration across the nation.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ecuador currently ranks 20, in most <a href="/wiki/World_Health_Organization_ranking_of_health_systems" class="mw-redirect" title="World Health Organization ranking of health systems">efficient health care countries</a>, compared to 111 back in the year 2000.<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup>  Ecuadorians have a life expectancy of 77.1 years.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> The infant mortality rate is 13 per 1,000 live births,<sup id="cite_ref-free_174-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-free-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> a major improvement from approximately 76 in the early 1980s and 140 in 1950.<sup id="cite_ref-cs_175-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-cs-175">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup> 23% of children under five are chronically malnourished.<sup id="cite_ref-free_174-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-free-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> Population in some rural areas have no access to potable water, and its supply is provided by mean of water tankers. There are 686 malaria cases per 100,000 people.<sup id="cite_ref-fightingdiseases.org_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-fightingdiseases.org-176">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> Basic health care, including doctor's visits, basic surgeries, and basic medications, has been provided free since 2008.<sup id="cite_ref-free_174-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-free-174">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> However, some public hospitals are in poor condition and often lack necessary supplies to attend the high demand of patients. Private hospitals and clinics are well equipped but still expensive for the majority of the population.
</p><p>Between 2008 and 2016, new public hospitals have been built. In 2008, the government introduced universal and compulsory social security coverage. In 2015, corruption remains a problem. Overbilling is recorded in 20% of public establishments and in 80% of private establishments.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Education">Education</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Education">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Education_in_Ecuador" title="Education in Ecuador">Education in Ecuador</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Quito_Observatory_04.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/Quito_Observatory_04.JPG/200px-Quito_Observatory_04.JPG" decoding="async" width="200" height="150" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/Quito_Observatory_04.JPG/300px-Quito_Observatory_04.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/Quito_Observatory_04.JPG/400px-Quito_Observatory_04.JPG 2x" data-file-width="4608" data-file-height="3456" /></a><figcaption>The oldest observatory in South America is the <a href="/wiki/Quito_Astronomical_Observatory" title="Quito Astronomical Observatory">Quito Astronomical Observatory</a>, founded in 1873 and located in <a href="/wiki/Quito" title="Quito">Quito</a>, Ecuador. The Quito Astronomical Observatory is managed by the <a href="/wiki/National_Polytechnic_School" class="mw-redirect" title="National Polytechnic School">National Polytechnic School</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>The Ecuadorian Constitution requires that all children attend school until they achieve a "basic level of education", which is estimated at nine school years.<sup id="cite_ref-ilab_179-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ilab-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> In 1996, the net primary enrollment rate was 96.9%, and 71.8% of children stayed in school until the fifth grade / age 10.<sup id="cite_ref-ilab_179-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ilab-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> The cost of primary and secondary education is borne by the government, but families often face significant additional expenses such as fees and transportation costs.<sup id="cite_ref-ilab_179-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ilab-179">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Provision of public schools falls far below the levels needed, and class sizes are often very large, and families of limited means often find it necessary to pay for education.<sup id="cite_ref-180" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-180">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> In rural areas, only 10% of the children go on to high school.<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> In a 2015 report, The Ministry of Education states that in 2014 the mean number of school years completed in rural areas is 7.39 as compared to 10.86 in urban areas.<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Largest_cities">Largest cities</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=35" title="Edit section: Largest cities">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Ecuador" title="List of cities in Ecuador">List of cities in Ecuador</a></div>
<p>The five largest cities in the country are <a href="/wiki/Quito" title="Quito">Quito</a> (2.78 million inhabitants), <a href="/wiki/Guayaquil" title="Guayaquil">Guayaquil</a> (2.72 million inhabitants), <a href="/wiki/Cuenca,_Ecuador" title="Cuenca, Ecuador">Cuenca</a> (636,996 inhabitants), <a href="/wiki/Santo_Domingo_(Ecuador)" class="mw-redirect" title="Santo Domingo (Ecuador)">Santo Domingo</a> (458,580 inhabitants), and <a href="/wiki/Ambato,_Ecuador" title="Ambato, Ecuador">Ambato</a> (387,309 inhabitants). The most populated metropolitan areas of the country are those of Guayaquil, Quito, Cuenca, Manabí Centro (<a href="/wiki/Portoviejo" title="Portoviejo">Portoviejo</a>-<a href="/wiki/Manta,_Ecuador" title="Manta, Ecuador">Manta</a>) and Ambato.<sup id="cite_ref-Proyecciones_Poblacionales_183-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Proyecciones_Poblacionales-183">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1148739982">.mw-parser-output .largestCities-table-background{background:#f9f9f9}.mw-parser-output .largestCities-cell-background{background:#f0f0f0}</style>
</p>
<div>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Immigration_and_emigration">Immigration and emigration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=36" title="Edit section: Immigration and emigration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Emigration_from_Ecuador" title="Emigration from Ecuador">Emigration from Ecuador</a></div>
<p>Ecuador houses a small East Asian community mainly consisting of those of <a href="/wiki/Japanese_people" title="Japanese people">Japanese</a> and <a href="/wiki/Chinese_people" title="Chinese people">Chinese</a> descent, whose ancestors arrived as miners, farmhands and fishermen in the late 19th century.<sup id="cite_ref-CIA_2-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CIA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the early years of World War II, Ecuador still admitted a certain number of immigrants, and in 1939, when several South American countries refused to accept 165 <a href="/wiki/Jewish" class="mw-redirect" title="Jewish">Jewish</a> refugees from Germany aboard the ship <i>Koenigstein</i>, Ecuador granted them entry permits.<sup id="cite_ref-184" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-184">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Migration from <a href="/wiki/Lebanon" title="Lebanon">Lebanon</a> to Ecuador started as early as 1875.<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> Early impoverished migrants tended to work as independent sidewalk vendors, rather than as wage workers in agriculture or others' businesses.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup> Though they emigrated to escape Ottoman Turkish religious oppression, they were called "Turks" by Ecuadorians because they carried Ottoman passports.<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup> There were further waves of immigration in the first half of the 20th century; by 1930, there were 577 Lebanese immigrants and 489 of their descendants residing in the country. A 1986 estimate from Lebanon's Ministry of Foreign Affairs stated 100,000 Lebanese descendants.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup> They reside mostly in Quito and Guayaquil. They are predominantly <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholics</a>.
</p><p>In the early 1900s there was immigration from <a href="/wiki/Italians" title="Italians">Italians</a>, <a href="/wiki/Germans" title="Germans">Germans</a>, <a href="/wiki/Portuguese_people" title="Portuguese people">Portuguese</a>, <a href="/wiki/French_people" title="French people">French</a>, <a href="/wiki/Britons" class="mw-redirect" title="Britons">Britons</a>, <a href="/wiki/Irish_people" title="Irish people">Irish</a> and <a href="/wiki/Greeks" title="Greeks">Greeks</a>. The town of <a href="/wiki/Anc%C3%B3n,_Ecuador" title="Ancón, Ecuador">Ancón</a> experienced of wave of immigration from the UK starting in 1911, when the Government of Ecuador conceded 98 mines, occupying an area of 38,842 hectares, to the British oil company Anglo Ecuadorian Oilfields. Today, the Anglo American Oilfields or <a href="/wiki/Anglo_American_plc" title="Anglo American plc">Anglo American plc</a> is the world's largest producer of platinum, with around 40% of world output, as well as being a major producer of diamonds, copper, nickel, iron ore and steelmaking coal. <a href="/wiki/Alberto_Spencer" title="Alberto Spencer">Alberto Spencer</a> is one famed Briton that hailed from Ancon. The town has now become an attraction due to the austere British homes in "El Barrio Ingles" situated in a contrasting tropical setting.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the 1950s the <a href="/wiki/Italians" title="Italians">Italians</a> were the third largest national group in terms of numbers of immigrants. It can be noted that, after World War I, people from <a href="/wiki/Liguria" title="Liguria">Liguria</a>, still constituted the majority of the flow, even though they then represented only one third of the total number of immigrants in Ecuador. This situation came from the improvement of the economic situation in Liguria. The classic paradigm of the Italian immigrant today was not that of the small trader from Liguria as it had been before; those who emigrated to Ecuador were professionals and technicians, employees and religious people from South-Central Italy. It must be remembered that many immigrants, a remarkable number of Italians among them, moved to the Ecuadorian port from Peru to escape from the Peruvian war with Chile. The Italian government came to be more interested in the emigration phenomenon in Ecuador because of the necessity of finding an outlet for the large number of immigrants who traditionally went to the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a> but who could no longer enter this country because of the <a href="/wiki/Emergency_Quota_Act" title="Emergency Quota Act">Emergency Quota Act</a> of 1921 that restricted immigration of Southern and Eastern Europeans as well as other "undesirables".
</p><p>Most of these communities and their descendants are located in the <a href="/wiki/Guayas_Province" title="Guayas Province">Guayas</a> region of the country.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Throughout the 20th century, immigration also came from other <a href="/wiki/Latin_American" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin American">Latin American</a> countries due to civil wars, economic crises, and dictatorships. The most notable are those coming from <a href="/wiki/Argentina" title="Argentina">Argentina</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chile" title="Chile">Chile</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Uruguay" title="Uruguay">Uruguay</a>. Starting from 2002, there has been an exponential and significant growth in <a href="/wiki/Colombians" title="Colombians">Colombian</a> and <a href="/wiki/Venezuelan" class="mw-redirect" title="Venezuelan">Venezuelan</a> refugees. Colombians have historically found refuge in its neighboring country during times of civil unrest. Recently, Venezuelans have become a notable presence in Ecuadorian cities as many flee the economic and political <a href="/wiki/Venezuelan_crisis" class="mw-redirect" title="Venezuelan crisis">Venezuelan crisis</a>. Authorities argue that an estimated 350,000 to 400,000 Colombians live in Ecuador, porous borders and lack of formal registration disallow concrete numbers.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:CUENCA_(32097469484).jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/CUENCA_%2832097469484%29.jpg/220px-CUENCA_%2832097469484%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/CUENCA_%2832097469484%29.jpg/330px-CUENCA_%2832097469484%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/42/CUENCA_%2832097469484%29.jpg/440px-CUENCA_%2832097469484%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2800" data-file-height="1867" /></a><figcaption>Historic center of <a href="/wiki/Cuenca,_Ecuador" title="Cuenca, Ecuador">Cuenca</a>, a UNESCO World Heritage Site</figcaption></figure>
<p>From 2007, the Ecuador government created multiple initiatives to attract Ecuadorians abroad mostly from the <a href="/wiki/United_States" title="United States">United States</a>, <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a> to return after many left during the 90s economic crisis or <a href="/wiki/La_Decada_Perdida" class="mw-redirect" title="La Decada Perdida">La Decada Perdida</a>. These policies resulted in the a rapid and significant rise in the flow of returning nationals, most notably during the 2008 economic crisis that affected Europe and North America.<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup>
In recent years,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items" title="Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers"><span title="The time period mentioned near this tag is ambiguous. (July 2023)">when?</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Ecuador has grown in popularity among North American expatriates.<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Culture">Culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=37" title="Edit section: Culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Ecuador" title="Culture of Ecuador">Culture of Ecuador</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Ca%C3%B1ari_people_01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Ca%C3%B1ari_people_01.jpg/170px-Ca%C3%B1ari_people_01.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="238" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Ca%C3%B1ari_people_01.jpg/255px-Ca%C3%B1ari_people_01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/17/Ca%C3%B1ari_people_01.jpg/340px-Ca%C3%B1ari_people_01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2194" data-file-height="3072" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Ca%C3%B1ari" title="Cañari">Cañari</a> children with the typical Andean indigenous clothes</figcaption></figure>
<p>Ecuador's mainstream culture is defined by its <i><a href="/wiki/Mestizo" title="Mestizo">mestizo</a></i> majority, and, like their ancestry, it is traditionally of Spanish heritage, influenced in different degrees by Amerindian traditions and in some cases by African elements. The first and most substantial wave of modern immigration to Ecuador consisted of Spanish colonists, following the arrival of Europeans in 1499. A lower number of other Europeans and North Americans migrated to the country in the late 19th and early 20th centuries and, in smaller numbers, Poles, Lithuanians, English, Irish, and Croats during and after the Second World War.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-right" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Alicia_Cahuiya,_Caso_Tagaeri_y_Taromenani.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Alicia_Cahuiya%2C_Caso_Tagaeri_y_Taromenani.jpg/170px-Alicia_Cahuiya%2C_Caso_Tagaeri_y_Taromenani.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="113" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Alicia_Cahuiya%2C_Caso_Tagaeri_y_Taromenani.jpg/255px-Alicia_Cahuiya%2C_Caso_Tagaeri_y_Taromenani.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Alicia_Cahuiya%2C_Caso_Tagaeri_y_Taromenani.jpg/340px-Alicia_Cahuiya%2C_Caso_Tagaeri_y_Taromenani.jpg 2x" data-file-width="5520" data-file-height="3680" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Huaorani_people" title="Huaorani people">Huaorani</a> man with the typical Amazonian indigenous clothes</figcaption></figure>
<p>Ecuador's Amerindian communities are integrated into the mainstream culture to varying degrees,<sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> but some may also practice their own native cultures, particularly the more remote Amerindian communities of the <a href="/wiki/Amazon_basin" title="Amazon basin">Amazon basin</a>. Spanish is spoken as the first language by more than 90% of the population and as a first or second language by more than 98%. Part of Ecuador's population can speak <a href="/wiki/Amerindian" class="mw-redirect" title="Amerindian">Amerindian</a> languages, in some cases as a second language. Two percent of the population speak only Amerindian languages.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=38" title="Edit section: Music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Music_of_Ecuador" title="Music of Ecuador">Music of Ecuador</a></div>
<p>The music of Ecuador has a long history. <a href="/wiki/Pasillo" title="Pasillo">Pasillo</a> is a genre of indigenous Latin music. In Ecuador it is the "national genre of music". Through the years, many cultures have brought their influences together to create new types of music. There are also different kinds of traditional music like albazo, pasacalle, fox incaico, tonada, capishca, <a href="/wiki/Bomba_(Ecuador)" title="Bomba (Ecuador)">Bomba</a> (highly established in Afro-Ecuadorian societies), and so on. <a href="/wiki/Tecnocumbia" title="Tecnocumbia">Tecnocumbia</a> and Rockola are clear examples of the influence of foreign cultures. One of the most traditional forms of dancing in Ecuador is <a href="/w/index.php?title=Sanjuanito&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Sanjuanito (page does not exist)">Sanjuanito</a>. It is originally from northern Ecuador (<a href="/wiki/Otavalo_(city)" title="Otavalo (city)">Otavalo</a>-Imbabura). Sanjuanito is a type of dance music played during festivities by the mestizo and Amerindian communities. According to the Ecuadorian musicologist Segundo Luis Moreno, Sanjuanito was danced by Amerindian people during San Juan Bautista's birthday. This important date was established by the Spaniards on 24 June, coincidentally the same date when Amerindian people celebrated their rituals of <a href="/wiki/Inti_Raymi" title="Inti Raymi">Inti Raymi</a>.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Cuisine">Cuisine</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=39" title="Edit section: Cuisine">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/List_of_Ecuadorian_dishes_and_foods" title="List of Ecuadorian dishes and foods">List of Ecuadorian dishes and foods</a></div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1096954695/mw-parser-output/.tmulti">.mw-parser-output .tmulti .multiimageinner{display:flex;flex-direction:column}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .thumbcaption{text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .tmulti .trow>.thumbcaption{text-align:center}}</style><div class="thumb tmulti tright"><div class="thumbinner multiimageinner" //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e1/Ceviche_de_camar%C3%B3n_%28gastronom%C3%ADa_Ecuatoriana%29.jpg/300px-Ceviche_de_camar%C3%B3n_%28gastronom%C3%ADa_Ecuatoriana%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1032" data-file-height="581" /></a></span></div></div><div class="tsingle" style="width:156px;max-width:156px"><div class="thumbimage"><span typeof="mw:File"><a href="/wiki/File:Cuy_Guinea_Pig_Dish_SG.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Cuy_Guinea_Pig_Dish_SG.jpg/154px-Cuy_Guinea_Pig_Dish_SG.jpg" decoding="async" width="154" height="126" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Cuy_Guinea_Pig_Dish_SG.jpg/231px-Cuy_Guinea_Pig_Dish_SG.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1c/Cuy_Guinea_Pig_Dish_SG.jpg/308px-Cuy_Guinea_Pig_Dish_SG.jpg 2x" data-file-width="558" data-file-height="455" /></a></span></div></div></div><div class="trow" style="display:flex"><div class="thumbcaption"><i>Ceviche ecuatoriano</i> (Ecuadorian-style <a href="/wiki/Ceviche" title="Ceviche">ceviche</a>) and <i>Cuy asado</i> (grilled <a href="/wiki/Guinea_pig" title="Guinea pig">guinea pig</a>) are some of the typical dishes.</div></div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Ecuadorian_cuisine" title="Ecuadorian cuisine">Ecuadorian cuisine</a> is diverse, varying with the altitude, associated agricultural conditions, and ethnic / racial communities. Most regions in Ecuador follow the traditional three-course meal of soup, a course that includes rice and a protein, and then dessert and coffee to finish.
</p><p>In the coastal region, seafood is very popular, with fish, shrimp, and <i><a href="/wiki/Ceviche" title="Ceviche">ceviche</a></i> being an integral part of the diet. Beef is also notably consumed in the coastal region, traditional dishes are <a href="/wiki/Churrasco" title="Churrasco">churrasco</a> and arroz con menestra y carne asada (rice with beans and grilled beef) served with <a href="/wiki/Tostones" title="Tostones">fried plantain</a>. The latter is an emblematic dish of the city of <a href="/wiki/Guayaquil" title="Guayaquil">Guayaquil</a>. Meat based dishes have their origins in the cattle ranching culture of the <a href="/wiki/Montubio" title="Montubio">Montubio</a> people.
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Ceviche" title="Ceviche">Ceviche</a> is an indispensable coastal dish with pre-incan origins. It is often served with fried plantain (<a href="/wiki/Chifle" title="Chifle">chifles</a> or patacones), popcorn, or <a href="/wiki/Tostada_(tortilla)" title="Tostada (tortilla)">tostado</a>. <a href="/wiki/Plantain_(cooking)" class="mw-redirect" title="Plantain (cooking)">Plantain</a>- and peanut-based dishes are quite frequent in the coastal region reflecting the West African roots of many of its citizens. Encocados (dishes that contain a coconut sauce) are also very popular in the northern coast centering around the city of <a href="/wiki/Esmeraldas,_Ecuador" title="Esmeraldas, Ecuador">Esmeraldas</a>. The coast is also a leading producer of <a href="/wiki/Banana" title="Banana">bananas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cocoa_bean" title="Cocoa bean">cocoa beans</a> (to make chocolate), shrimp, <a href="/wiki/Tilapia" title="Tilapia">tilapia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mango" title="Mango">mango</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Passiflora_edulis" title="Passiflora edulis">passion fruit</a>, among other products.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2014)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Pan_de_yuca" title="Pan de yuca">Pan de yuca</a>, analogous with the Brazilian <a href="/wiki/P%C3%A3o_de_queijo" title="Pão de queijo">pão de queijo</a>, is served with "yogur persa" and is often eaten as a snack in many coastal cities. Its origin comes from the Persian and Middle Eastern populations that settled the coast.<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the highland region, various dishes of pork, chicken, and <i>cuy</i> (guinea pig)<sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup> are popular and are served with a variety of grains (especially rice and <a href="/wiki/Mote_(food)" title="Mote (food)">mote</a>) or potatoes.<sup id="cite_ref-199" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-199">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> The consumption of "Cuy" or <a href="/wiki/Guinea_Pig" class="mw-redirect" title="Guinea Pig">Guinea Pig</a>, de rigueur in mostly indigenous communities, reflects the predominantly native character of the highlands. Considered a delicacy it is often characterized as having a mild pork flavor. 
</p><p>In the Amazon region, a dietary staple is the <i>yuca</i>, elsewhere called <a href="/wiki/Cassava" title="Cassava">cassava</a>. Many fruits are available in this region, including <a href="/wiki/Banana" title="Banana">bananas</a>, <a href="/wiki/Cyphostemma_juttae" title="Cyphostemma juttae">tree grapes</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Bactris_gasipaes" title="Bactris gasipaes">peach palms</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Literature">Literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=40" title="Edit section: Literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Jmontalvo.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Jmontalvo.jpg/170px-Jmontalvo.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="234" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Jmontalvo.jpg/255px-Jmontalvo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0b/Jmontalvo.jpg/340px-Jmontalvo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="439" data-file-height="603" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Juan_Montalvo" title="Juan Montalvo">Juan Montalvo</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>Early literature in colonial Ecuador, as in the rest of Spanish America, was influenced by the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Golden_Age" title="Spanish Golden Age">Spanish Golden Age</a>. One of the earliest examples is <a href="/wiki/Jacinto_Collahuazo" title="Jacinto Collahuazo">Jacinto Collahuazo</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Boceto_201-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Boceto-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> an Amerindian chief of a northern village in today's Ibarra, born in the late 1600s. Despite the early repression and discrimination of the native people by the Spanish, Collahuazo learned to read and write in <a href="/wiki/Spanish_language" title="Spanish language">Castilian</a>, but his work was written in <a href="/wiki/Quechua_languages" class="mw-redirect" title="Quechua languages">Quechua</a>. The use of <a href="/wiki/Quipu" title="Quipu">Quipu</a> was banned by the Spanish,<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup> and in order to preserve their work, many Inca poets had to resort to the use of the Latin alphabet to write in their native Quechua language. The history behind the Inca drama "Ollantay", the oldest literary piece in existence for any Amerindian language in America,<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> shares some similarities with the work of Collahuazo. Collahuazo was imprisoned and all of his work burned. The existence of his literary work came to light many centuries later, when a crew of masons was restoring the walls of a colonial church in Quito and found a hidden manuscript. The salvaged fragment is a Spanish translation from Quechua of the "Elegy to the Dead of Atahualpa",<sup id="cite_ref-Boceto_201-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Boceto-201">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> a poem written by Collahuazo, which describes the sadness and impotence of the Inca people of having lost their king Atahualpa.
</p><p>Other early Ecuadorian writers include the Jesuits <a href="/wiki/Juan_Bautista_Aguirre" title="Juan Bautista Aguirre">Juan Bautista Aguirre</a>, born in <a href="/wiki/Daule_Canton" title="Daule Canton">Daule</a> in 1725, and Father <a href="/wiki/Juan_de_Velasco" title="Juan de Velasco">Juan de Velasco</a>, born in Riobamba in 1727. Famous authors from the late colonial and early republic period include <a href="/wiki/Eugenio_Espejo" title="Eugenio Espejo">Eugenio Espejo</a>, a printer and main author of the first newspaper in Ecuadorian colonial times; <a href="/wiki/Jose_Joaquin_de_Olmedo" class="mw-redirect" title="Jose Joaquin de Olmedo">Jose Joaquin de Olmedo</a> (born in Guayaquil), famous for his ode to <a href="/wiki/Sim%C3%B3n_Bol%C3%ADvar" title="Simón Bolívar">Simón Bolívar</a> titled <i>Victoria de Junin</i>; <a href="/wiki/Juan_Montalvo" title="Juan Montalvo">Juan Montalvo</a>, a prominent essayist and novelist; <a href="/wiki/Juan_Leon_Mera" class="mw-redirect" title="Juan Leon Mera">Juan Leon Mera</a>, famous for his work "Cumanda" or "Tragedy among Savages" and the Ecuadorian National Anthem; Juan A. Martinez with <i>A la Costa</i>; Dolores Veintimilla;<sup id="cite_ref-204" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-204">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> and others.
</p><p>Contemporary Ecuadorian writers include the novelist <a href="/wiki/Jorge_Enrique_Adoum" title="Jorge Enrique Adoum">Jorge Enrique Adoum</a>; the poet <a href="/wiki/Jorge_Carrera_Andrade" title="Jorge Carrera Andrade">Jorge Carrera Andrade</a>; the essayist <a href="/wiki/Benjam%C3%ADn_Carri%C3%B3n" title="Benjamín Carrión">Benjamín Carrión</a>; the poets Medardo Angel Silva, Jorge Carrera Andrade, and <a href="/wiki/Luis_Costales" class="mw-redirect" title="Luis Costales">Luis Alberto Costales</a>; the novelist Enrique Gil Gilbert; the novelist <a href="/wiki/Jorge_Icaza" class="mw-redirect" title="Jorge Icaza">Jorge Icaza</a> (author of the novel <i>Huasipungo</i>, translated to many languages); the short story author Pablo Palacio; and the novelist Alicia Yanez Cossio.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Art">Art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=41" title="Edit section: Art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Yapanga_de_Quito_con_traje_que_usa_esta_clase_de_mujeres_que_tratan_de_agradar_01.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Yapanga_de_Quito_con_traje_que_usa_esta_clase_de_mujeres_que_tratan_de_agradar_01.jpg/220px-Yapanga_de_Quito_con_traje_que_usa_esta_clase_de_mujeres_que_tratan_de_agradar_01.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="169" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Yapanga_de_Quito_con_traje_que_usa_esta_clase_de_mujeres_que_tratan_de_agradar_01.jpg/330px-Yapanga_de_Quito_con_traje_que_usa_esta_clase_de_mujeres_que_tratan_de_agradar_01.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/52/Yapanga_de_Quito_con_traje_que_usa_esta_clase_de_mujeres_que_tratan_de_agradar_01.jpg/440px-Yapanga_de_Quito_con_traje_que_usa_esta_clase_de_mujeres_que_tratan_de_agradar_01.jpg 2x" data-file-width="3542" data-file-height="2722" /></a><figcaption>Ecuador has a wide repertoire of colonial art in its churches and museums, like this: "<i>Yapanga from Quito in a suit worn by this class of women who try to please</i>" (1783) by <a href="/wiki/Vicente_Alb%C3%A1n" title="Vicente Albán">Vicente Albán</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;205&#93;</a></sup></figcaption></figure>
<p>The best known art styles from Ecuador belonged to the <i><a href="/wiki/Escuela_Quite%C3%B1a" class="mw-redirect" title="Escuela Quiteña">Escuela Quiteña</a></i> (Quito School), which developed from the 16th to 18th centuries, examples of which are on display in various old churches in Quito. <a href="/wiki/Ecuadorian_painters" class="mw-redirect" title="Ecuadorian painters">Ecuadorian painters</a> include <a href="/wiki/Eduardo_Kingman" title="Eduardo Kingman">Eduardo Kingman</a>, <a href="/wiki/Oswaldo_Guayasam%C3%ADn" title="Oswaldo Guayasamín">Oswaldo Guayasamín</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Camilo_Egas" title="Camilo Egas">Camilo Egas</a> from the Indiginist Movement; <a href="/wiki/Manuel_Rendon" title="Manuel Rendon">Manuel Rendon</a>, Jaime Zapata, <a href="/wiki/Enrique_T%C3%A1bara" title="Enrique Tábara">Enrique Tábara</a>, <a href="/wiki/An%C3%ADbal_Villac%C3%ADs" title="Aníbal Villacís">Aníbal Villacís</a>, <a href="/wiki/Theo_Constant%C3%A9" title="Theo Constanté">Theo Constanté</a>, <a href="/wiki/Luis_Molinari" title="Luis Molinari">Luis Molinari</a>, <a href="/wiki/Araceli_Gilbert" title="Araceli Gilbert">Araceli Gilbert</a>, <a href="/wiki/Judith_Gutierrez" class="mw-redirect" title="Judith Gutierrez">Judith Gutierrez</a>, <a href="/wiki/F%C3%A9lix_Arauz" title="Félix Arauz">Félix Arauz</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Estuardo_Maldonado" title="Estuardo Maldonado">Estuardo Maldonado</a> from the Informalist Movement; <a href="/wiki/Teddy_Cobe%C3%B1a" title="Teddy Cobeña">Teddy Cobeña</a> from expressionism and figurative style<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;206&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;207&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;208&#93;</a></sup> and Luis Burgos Flor with his abstract, futuristic style. The Amerindian people of Tigua, Ecuador, are also world-renowned for their <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Ecuador#Indigenous_art_of_Tigua" title="Culture of Ecuador">traditional paintings</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;209&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Sports">Sports</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Ecuador&amp;action=edit&amp;section=42" title="Edit section: Sports">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Sport_in_Ecuador" title="Sport in Ecuador">Sport in Ecuador</a></div>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Estadio_Monumental_6.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Estadio_Monumental_6.JPG/220px-Estadio_Monumental_6.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="144" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Estadio_Monumental_6.JPG/330px-Estadio_Monumental_6.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Estadio_Monumental_6.JPG/440px-Estadio_Monumental_6.JPG 2x" data-file-width="2000" data-file-height="1312" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Estadio_Monumental_Isidro_Romero_Carbo" title="Estadio Monumental Isidro Romero Carbo">Estadio Monumental Isidro Romero Carbo</a> of Guayaquil</figcaption></figure>
<p>The most popular <a href="/wiki/Sport_in_Ecuador" title="Sport in Ecuador">sport in Ecuador</a>, as in most South American countries, is football. Its best known professional teams include; <a href="/wiki/Club_Sport_Emelec" class="mw-redirect" title="Club Sport Emelec">Emelec</a> from <a href="/wiki/Guayaquil" title="Guayaquil">Guayaquil</a>, <a href="/wiki/LDU_Quito" class="mw-redirect" title="LDU Quito">Liga De Quito</a> from <a href="/wiki/Quito" title="Quito">Quito</a>; <a href="/wiki/Barcelona_S.C." title="Barcelona S.C.">Barcelona S.C.</a> from <a href="/wiki/Guayaquil" title="Guayaquil">Guayaquil</a>, the most popular team in Ecuador, also the team with most local championships; <a href="/wiki/Sociedad_Deportivo_Quito" class="mw-redirect" title="Sociedad Deportivo Quito">Deportivo Quito</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Club_Deportivo_El_Nacional" class="mw-redirect" title="Club Deportivo El Nacional">El Nacional</a> from Quito; <a href="/wiki/Centro_Deportivo_Olmedo" class="mw-redirect" title="Centro Deportivo Olmedo">Olmedo</a> from <a href="/wiki/Riobamba" title="Riobamba">Riobamba</a>; and <a href="/wiki/Club_Deportivo_Cuenca" class="mw-redirect" title="Club Deportivo Cuenca">Deportivo Cuenca</a> from Cuenca. Currently the most successful football team in Ecuador is LDU Quito, and it is the only Ecuadorian team that has won the <i><a href="/wiki/Copa_Libertadores" title="Copa Libertadores">Copa Libertadores</a></i>, the <i><a href="/wiki/Copa_Sudamericana" title="Copa Sudamericana">Copa Sudamericana</a></i>, and the <i><a href="/wiki/Recopa_Sudamericana" title="Recopa Sudamericana">Recopa Sudamericana</a></i>; they were also runners-up in the <a href="/wiki/2008_FIFA_Club_World_Cup" title="2008 FIFA Club World Cup">2008 FIFA Club World Cup</a>. The <a href="/wiki/Estadio_Monumental_Isidro_Romero_Carbo" title="Estadio Monumental Isidro Romero Carbo">Estadio Monumental Isidro Romero Carbo</a> is the tenth largest football stadium in South America. The <a href="/wiki/Ecuador_national_football_team" title="Ecuador national football team">Ecuador national football team</a> has appeared at four <a href="/wiki/FIFA_World_Cup" title="FIFA World Cup">FIFA World Cups</a>.
</p>

<li id="cite_note-realm-87"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-realm_87-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFTodd1974" class="citation book cs1">Todd, Ian (1974). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=gcEJAQAAIAAJ&amp;q=%22French+language+cultures%22+1974+pacific"><i>Island Realm: A Pacific Panorama</i></a>. Angus &amp; Robertson. p.&#160;190. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780207127618" title="Special:BookSources/9780207127618"><bdi>9780207127618</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">2 February</span> 2022</span>. <q>[we] can further define the word <i>culture</i> to mean <i>language</i>. Thus we have the French language part of Oceania, the Spanish part and the Japanese part. The Japanese culture groups of Oceania are the Bonin Islands, the Marcus Islands and the Volcano Islands. These three clusters, lying south and south-east of Japan, are inhabited either by Japanese or by people who have now completely fused with the Japanese race. Therefore they will not be taken into account in the proposed comparison of the policies of non – Oceanic cultures towards Oceanic peoples. On the eastern side of the Pacific are a number of Spanish language culture groups of islands. Two of them, the Galapagos and Easter Island, have been dealt with as separate chapters in this volume. Only one of the dozen or so Spanish culture island groups of Oceania has an Oceanic population — the Polynesians of Easter Island. The rest are either uninhabited or have a Spanish – Latin – American population consisting of people who migrated from the mainland. Therefore, the comparisons which follow refer almost exclusively to the English and French language cultures.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Island+Realm%3A+A+Pacific+Panorama&amp;rft.pages=190&amp;rft.pub=Angus+%26+Robertson&amp;rft.date=1974&amp;rft.isbn=9780207127618&amp;rft.aulast=Todd&amp;rft.aufirst=Ian&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DgcEJAQAAIAAJ%26q%3D%2522French%2Blanguage%2Bcultures%2522%2B1974%2Bpacific&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AEcuador" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-88"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-88">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite class="citation book cs1"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://wedocs.unep.org/bitstream/handle/20.500.11822/30152/RevOceania.pdf?sequence=1&amp;isAllowed=y"><i>Review of the Protected Areas System in Oceania</i></a> <span class="cs1-format">(PDF)</span>. International Union for Conservation of Nature and Natural Resources. 1986<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">17 January</span> 2022</span>. <q>Easter Island on the east has been included on the basis of its Polynesian and biogeographic affinities even though it is politically apart. The other islands of the eastern Pacific (Galapagos, Juan Fernandez, etc.) have sometimes been included in Oceania.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Review+of+the+Protected+Areas+System+in+Oceania&amp;rft.pub=International+Union+for+Conservation+of+Nature+and+Natural+Resources&amp;rft.date=1986&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fwedocs.unep.org%2Fbitstream%2Fhandle%2F20.500.11822%2F30152%2FRevOceania.pdf%3Fsequence%3D1%26isAllowed%3Dy&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AEcuador" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-89"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-89">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFHull1937" class="citation book cs1">Hull, Frank M. <li id="cite_note-90"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-90">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFEckertAlvaradoBalazsByles1998" class="citation book cs1">Eckert, Scott A.; Alvarado, Javier; Balazs, George H.; Byles, Richard; C. U.S. Department of Commerce, National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration, National Marine Fisheries Service. p.&#160;7. <q>Foraging hawksbills have been reported from virtually all of the island groups of Oceania, from the Galapagos Islands in the eastern Pacific to the Republic of Palau in the western Pacific.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Recovery+Plan+for+U.S.+Pacific+Populations+of+the+Hawksbill+Turtle+%28Eretmochelys+Imbricata%29&amp;rft.pages=7&amp;rft.pub=U.S.+Department+of+Commerce%2C+National+Oceanic+and+Atmospheric+Administration%2C+National+Marine+Fisheries+Service&amp;rft.date=1998&amp;rft.aulast=Eckert&amp;rft.aufirst=Scott+A.&amp;rft.au=Alvarado%2C+Javier&amp;rft.au=Balazs%2C+George+H.&amp;rft.au=Byles%2C+Richard&amp;rft.au=C.+Craig%2C+Peter&amp;rft.au=Dutton%2C+Peter+Howard&amp;rft.au=L.+Eckert%2C+Karen&amp;rft.au=Engbring%2C+John&amp;rft.au=Maragos%2C+James+E.&amp;rft.au=Pultz%2C+Susan&amp;rft.au=Richardson%2C+James+Ingram&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3Dsi33pkypsggC%26dq%3D%2522oceania%2522%2B%2522galapagos%2522%2B%2522indonesia%2522%26pg%3DPA7&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AEcuador" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-harv-91"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-harv_91-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFBequaert1941" class="citation book cs1">Bequaert, Joseph C. (1941). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://hbs.bishopmuseum.org/pubs-online/pdf/op16-11.pdf"><i>The Hippoboscidae of Oceania</i></a> <span class="cs1-format">(PDF)</span>. Harvard Medical School<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">24 January</span> 2022</span>. <q>In the present taxonomic study of the Hippoboscidae, Oceania covers, rather arbitrarily, the many archipelagos and isolated islands scattered throughout the Pacific Ocean, from the Marianas and Caroline Islands, the Bismarck Archipelago, the Solomon Islands and New Caledonia to the Hawaiian islands and the Galapagos.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=The+Hippoboscidae+of+Oceania&amp;rft.pub=Harvard+Medical+School&amp;rft.date=1941&amp;rft.aulast=Bequaert&amp;rft.aufirst=Joseph+C.&amp;rft_id=http%3A%2F%2Fhbs.bishopmuseum.org%2Fpubs-online%2Fpdf%2Fop16-11.pdf&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AEcuador" class="Z3988"></span></span>

<li id="cite_note-92"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-92">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFKing1976" class="citation book cs1">King, Frank P. (1976). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=tzMhAAAAMAAJ&amp;q=%22one+might+add+Chile+and+Ecuador%22"><i>Oceania and Beyond: Essays on the Pacific Since 1945</i></a>. Greenwood Press. pp.&#160;20–21. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780837189048" title="Special:BookSources/9780837189048"><bdi>9780837189048</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">7 February</span> 2022</span>. <q>It is clear that since World War II, Britain, in contrast to France and the United States (and one might say Chile and Ecuador, which hold, respectively, Easter Island and the Galapagos Islands), conceived of Oceania as a region of sovereign nations living in a spirit of commonwealth.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=Oceania+and+Beyond%3A+Essays+on+the+Pacific+Since+1945&amp;rft.pages=20-21&amp;rft.pub=Greenwood+Press&amp;rft.date=1976&amp;rft.isbn=9780837189048&amp;rft.aulast=King&amp;rft.aufirst=Frank+P.&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DtzMhAAAAMAAJ%26q%3D%2522one%2Bmight%2Badd%2BChile%2Band%2BEcuador%2522&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AEcuador" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-93"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-93">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFAldrich1993" class="citation book cs1">Aldrich, Robert (1993). <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://books.google.com/books?id=tLTDYaHhrZgC&amp;dq=%22oceania%22+%22galapagos%22+%22south+pacific%22&amp;pg=PA347"><i>France and the South Pacific Since 1940</i></a>. University of Hawaii Press. p.&#160;347. <a href="/wiki/ISBN_(identifier)" class="mw-redirect" title="ISBN (identifier)">ISBN</a>&#160;<a href="/wiki/Special:BookSources/9780824815585" title="Special:BookSources/9780824815585"><bdi>9780824815585</bdi></a><span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">18 February</span> 2022</span>. <q>Britain's high commissioner in New Zealand continues to administer Pitcairn, and the other former British colonies remain members of the Commonwealth of Nations, recognizing the British Queen as their titular head of state and vesting certain residual powers  in the British government or the Queen's representative in the islands. Australia did not cede control of the Torres Strait Islands, inhabited by a Melanesian population, or Lord Howe and Norfolk Island, whose residents are of European ancestry. New Zealand retains indirect rule over Niue and Tokelau and has kept close relations with another former possession, the Cook Islands, through a compact of free association. Chile rules Easter Island (Rapa Nui) and Ecuador rules the Galapagos Islands. The Aboriginals of Australia, the Maoris of New Zealand and the native Polynesians of Hawaii, despite movements demanding more cultural recognition, greater economic and political considerations or even outright sovereignty, have remained minorities in countries where massive waves of migration have completely changed society. In short, Oceania has remained one of the least completely decolonized regions on the globe.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Abook&amp;rft.genre=book&amp;rft.btitle=France+and+the+South+Pacific+Since+1940&amp;rft.pages=347&amp;rft.pub=University+of+Hawaii+Press&amp;rft.date=1993&amp;rft.isbn=9780824815585&amp;rft.aulast=Aldrich&amp;rft.aufirst=Robert&amp;rft_id=https%3A%2F%2Fbooks.google.com%2Fbooks%3Fid%3DtLTDYaHhrZgC%26dq%3D%2522oceania%2522%2B%2522galapagos%2522%2B%2522south%2Bpacific%2522%26pg%3DPA347&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3AEcuador" class="Z3988"></span></span>
</li>
